      explanation: Directly supports the fatal outcome of untreated IOPD from cardiopulmonary insufficiency.
- name: Absence of immunologically detectable GAA protein (CRIM-negative status)
  biological_scale: MOLECULAR
  description: >
    CRIM (cross-reactive immunologic material) status asks a different question from
    enzyme activity: not whether GAA works, but whether any GAA protein is made at
    all. Patients with two null alleles produce no GAA protein detectable by Western
    blot and are CRIM-negative; patients producing a catalytically inactive protein
    are CRIM-positive. Because a CRIM-negative infant has never been immunologically
    tolerized to GAA, recombinant human GAA is a foreign antigen. Roughly 20-25% of
    classic infantile patients are CRIM-negative, and CRIM status is largely
    predictable from the GAA genotype.
  gene:
    preferred_term: GAA
    description: Null GAA alleles producing no detectable protein define CRIM-negative status.
    modifier: DECREASED
    term:
      id: hgnc:4065
      label: GAA
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:22252923
    reference_title: "Predicting cross-reactive immunological material (CRIM) status in Pompe disease using GAA mutations: lessons learned from 10 years of clinical laboratory testing experience."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "CRIM-negative patients make no GAA protein and develop sustained high antibody titers to ERT that render the treatment ineffective."
    explanation: Defines CRIM-negative status as absence of GAA protein and links it to the antibody response.
  - reference: PMID:19775921
    reference_title: "Cross-reactive immunologic material status affects treatment outcomes in Pompe disease infants."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Patients synthesize a non-functional form of GAA or are unable to form native enzyme."
    explanation: States the CRIM-positive versus CRIM-negative molecular distinction.
  downstream:
  - target: High sustained anti-rhGAA antibody response
    description: >
      Never having been tolerized to endogenous GAA, CRIM-negative infants mount an
      early, high-titer, sustained IgG response to infused recombinant human GAA.
    causal_link_type: DIRECT
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:19775921
      reference_title: "Cross-reactive immunologic material status affects treatment outcomes in Pompe disease infants."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "IgG antibodies to rhGAA developed earlier and serotiters were higher and more sustained in the CRIM-negative group."
      explanation: Directly supports the CRIM-negative-to-antibody-response edge with comparative titer data.
    - reference: PMID:20301438
      reference_title: "Pompe Disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: OTHER
      snippet: "individuals who do not produce CRIM (i.e., who are CRIM negative) generally develop high titer anti-rhGAA antibodies during ERT"
      explanation: Management guidance confirms CRIM-negative status predicts a high-titer anti-rhGAA response.
- name: High sustained anti-rhGAA antibody response
  biological_scale: ORGANISM
  description: >
    Sustained high-titer neutralizing IgG against recombinant human GAA blocks the
    therapeutic benefit of enzyme replacement therapy. This is a treatment-conditioned
    mechanism with no counterpart in late-onset disease, and it is the reason CRIM
    status must be determined before the first infusion.
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: humoral immune response mediated by circulating immunoglobulin
    modifier: INCREASED
    term:
      id: GO:0002455
      label: humoral immune response mediated by circulating immunoglobulin
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:22237443
    reference_title: "Successful immune tolerance induction to enzyme replacement therapy in CRIM-negative infantile Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Cross-reactive immunologic material negative (CRIM-negative) Pompe patients develop high-titer antibody to the rhGAA and do poorly."
    explanation: Establishes the high-titer antibody response and its adverse clinical consequence.
  downstream:
  - target: Enzyme replacement therapy failure and clinical decline
    description: Neutralizing antibody renders infused rhGAA ineffective, and treated CRIM-negative infants deteriorate.
    causal_link_type: DIRECT
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:19775921
      reference_title: "Cross-reactive immunologic material status affects treatment outcomes in Pompe disease infants."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "Following 52 weeks of treatment, 6/11 (54.5%) CRIM-negative and 1/21 (4.8%) CRIM-positive patients were deceased or invasively ventilated (p<0.0001)."
      explanation: Quantifies the treatment-failure outcome that follows the CRIM-negative antibody response.
    - reference: PMID:22252923
      reference_title: "Predicting cross-reactive immunological material (CRIM) status in Pompe disease using GAA mutations: lessons learned from 10 years of clinical laboratory testing experience."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "CRIM-negative patients make no GAA protein and develop sustained high antibody titers to ERT that render the treatment ineffective."
      explanation: States that the sustained antibody response renders ERT ineffective.
- name: Enzyme replacement therapy failure and clinical decline
  biological_scale: ORGANISM
  description: >
    In the absence of immune tolerance induction, CRIM-negative infants on ERT decline
    despite treatment: cardiac function and gross motor development improve
    significantly less than in CRIM-positive infants, and by roughly 27 months all
    CRIM-negative patients in the original series were dead or invasively ventilated.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:19775921
    reference_title: "Cross-reactive immunologic material status affects treatment outcomes in Pompe disease infants."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "By age 27.1 months, all CRIM-negative patients and 4/21 (19.0%) CRIM-positive patients were deceased or invasively ventilated."
    explanation: Quantifies the divergent treated outcome by CRIM status.
  - reference: PMID:19775921
    reference_title: "Cross-reactive immunologic material status affects treatment outcomes in Pompe disease infants."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Cardiac function and gross motor development improved significantly more in the CRIM-positive group."
    explanation: Shows the treatment benefit gap attributable to CRIM status.
- name: Failure of productive autophagy and autophagic buildup
  biological_scale: CELLULAR
  description: >
    Autophagy is lysosome-dependent, so the storage lesion disables it. Autophagic
    vesicles accumulate in type II-rich muscle fibers, disrupting the contractile
    apparatus. Critically for IOPD, the autophagic buildup also acts as a sink for
    infused recombinant enzyme and prevents its efficient delivery to lysosomes -
    a second, non-immune route to poor ERT response that runs in parallel to the
    CRIM-negative antibody arm.
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: Autophagy
    modifier: DECREASED
    term:
      id: GO:0006914
      label: autophagy
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: Skeletal muscle fiber
    term:
      id: CL:0008002
      label: skeletal muscle fiber
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:20040311
    reference_title: "Autophagy in skeletal muscle: implications for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "we present evidence that a failure of productive autophagy in muscle tissue contributes strongly to disease pathology in both patients with Pompe disease and GAA-knockout mice"
    explanation: States that failed productive autophagy contributes to pathology in patients as well as in the knockout mouse.
  downstream:
  - target: Skeletal and respiratory myofiber injury
    description: Accumulated autophagosomes disrupt the contractile apparatus of muscle fibers.
    causal_link_type: DIRECT
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:20040311
      reference_title: "Autophagy in skeletal muscle: implications for Pompe disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
      snippet: "In the GAA-knockout mouse model, progressive accumulation of autophagic vesicles is restricted to Type II-rich muscle fibers."
      explanation: Localizes the autophagic buildup to the muscle fibers whose injury this edge asserts.
  - target: Enzyme replacement therapy failure and clinical decline
    description: >
      Autophagic buildup sequesters infused recombinant enzyme before it reaches the
      lysosome, degrading ERT response independently of any antibody response.
    causal_link_type: DIRECT
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:20040311
      reference_title: "Autophagy in skeletal muscle: implications for Pompe disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
      snippet: "Not only does this build-up of autophagosomes disrupt the contractile apparatus in the muscle fibers, it also interferes with enzyme replacement therapy by acting as a sink for the recombinant enzyme and preventing its efficient delivery to the lysosomes."
      explanation: Directly evidences the enzyme-sequestration route from autophagic buildup to reduced ERT efficacy.
- name: Central nervous system glycogen storage and progressive white matter abnormality
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  description: >
    Intravenous rhGAA does not cross the blood-brain barrier, so CNS glycogen storage
    is untreated. As ERT enables survival into later childhood and adulthood, slowly
    progressive symmetric white-matter abnormalities emerge - periventricular white
    matter and centrum semiovale from about age 2, then corpus callosum, capsules and
    subcortical areas after age 8, and brainstem from age 11 - with variable
    neuropsychological consequences. This is an ERT-era emerging phenotype rather than
    part of the classical untreated natural history.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:29573408
    reference_title: "Classic infantile Pompe patients approaching adulthood: a cohort study on consequences for the brain."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "From approximately age 2 years onwards, brain MRI showed involvement of the periventricular white matter and centrum semiovale. After 8 years of age, additional white-matter abnormalities occurred in the corpus callosum, internal and external capsule, and subcortical areas. From 11 years of age, white-matter abnormalities were also found in the brainstem."
    explanation: Documents the anatomical progression of white-matter abnormality in treated classic infantile patients.
  downstream:
  - target: Cognitive decline and intellectual disability
    description: White-matter involvement is accompanied by neuropsychological decline in a subset of long-term survivors.
    causal_link_type: INDIRECT_UNKNOWN_INTERMEDIATES
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:29573408
      reference_title: "Classic infantile Pompe patients approaching adulthood: a cohort study on consequences for the brain."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "In our long-term survivors treated intravenously with enzyme replacement therapy, we found slowly progressive symmetric white-matter abnormalities. Cognitive development varied from stable and normal to declines towards intellectual disabilities."
      explanation: Directly links progressive white-matter abnormality to the range of cognitive outcomes observed.
phenotypes:
- name: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy
  category: Cardiovascular
  description: >
    Cardiomegaly with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, usually identifiable in the first
    weeks of life and progressing to left ventricular outflow obstruction. Its
    presence in an infant with GAA deficiency is what defines classic infantile
    disease rather than late-onset disease.
  frequency: OBLIGATE

    explanation: Cardiomyopathy is definitional for IOPD, supporting an obligate frequency.

- name: Cardiomegaly

    explanation: Reports cardiomegaly in 92% of the 168-patient untreated cohort, the highest frequency of any sign listed, which is the basis for the VERY_FREQUENT band.

histopathology:
- name: Vacuolar myopathy with lysosomal glycogen accumulation
  description: >-
    Skeletal muscle in classic infantile-onset disease shows a vacuolar myopathy in
    which glycogen fills the lysosomes of myofibers. The storage lesion is not
    confined to muscle: it also involves cardiac and smooth muscle, which is why the
    same biopsy-level mechanism underlies both the cardiac and the skeletal-muscle
    arms of the disease. Muscle biopsy is no longer the diagnostic route - enzyme
    assay and GAA sequencing are - but it remains the tissue-level readout used to
    judge whether enzyme replacement therapy is reaching muscle.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:17075580
    reference_title: "Characterization of pre- and post-treatment pathology after enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "glycogen accumulates abnormally in the lysosomes of skeletal, cardiac and smooth muscle, and contributes to clinically progressive and debilitating muscle weakness"
    explanation: States the tissue distribution of the lysosomal glycogen storage lesion and links it to the weakness.
- name: Variable glycogen clearance on enzyme replacement therapy
  description: >-
    Serial quadriceps biopsies taken at baseline, 12 weeks, and 52 weeks in eight
    infantile-onset patients on weekly recombinant human GAA showed that the extent
    of glycogen clearance differed widely between patients and tracked clinical
    outcome. This is the histologic counterpart of the entry's clinical observation
    that enzyme replacement therapy rescues some infants and not others, and it makes
    the biopsy an outcome measure rather than only a diagnostic specimen.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:17075580
    reference_title: "Characterization of pre- and post-treatment pathology after enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The extent of glycogen clearance varied widely among these patient samples, and correlated well with clinical outcome"
    explanation: Establishes that histologic glycogen clearance varies between treated infants and predicts how they do clinically.
- name: Baseline histologic predictors of enzyme replacement response
  description: >-
    In the same series, low baseline glycogen, mild ultrastructural damage, a high
    proportion of type I fibers, and young age at baseline all marked patients who
    responded well histologically. The first two are a statement that the amount of
    damage already present limits what enzyme replacement can undo, which is the
    tissue-level argument for treating before symptoms rather than after.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:17075580
    reference_title: "Characterization of pre- and post-treatment pathology after enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Low glycogen levels, mild ultrastructural damage, a high proportion of type I fibers, and young age at baseline were all features associated with good histologic response"
    explanation: Names the baseline biopsy features that predict a good histologic response to enzyme replacement therapy.
  - reference: PMID:17075580
    reference_title: "Characterization of pre- and post-treatment pathology after enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "There was no correlation between capillary density and glycogen clearance"
    explanation: Reports a negative result - delivery of enzyme to muscle is not limited by capillary supply - which narrows the candidate explanations for poor response.
- name: Increased activated satellite cells in poorly responding post-treatment biopsies
  description: >-
    Confocal analysis of the post-treatment biopsies found higher levels of activated
    satellite cells in those with a poor histologic response. The finding is
    correlative, and the study does not establish whether the raised satellite-cell
    activation is a compensatory regenerative response to continuing myofiber injury
    or part of what limits the response.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:17075580
    reference_title: "Characterization of pre- and post-treatment pathology after enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "activated satellite cell levels were shown to be higher in post-treatment biopsies with poor histologic responses"
    explanation: Reports the satellite-cell measurement and the direction of its association with histologic response.
- name: Widespread CNS glycogen storage with white-matter and deep grey-nucleus predominance
  description: >-
    A systematic review of 39 autopsy-based articles covering 49 patients meeting a
    strict classic-infantile definition (hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, onset under 12
    months, GAA deficiency) found histologic abnormality throughout the central
    nervous system. Cerebral and cerebellar white matter, globus pallidus, dentate
    nucleus, brainstem motor nuclei, and anterior horn cells were affected most
    often, and subcortical structures more consistently than cortical ones. This is
    the tissue-level counterpart of the white-matter abnormality curated as a
    phenotype and pathophysiology node in this entry, which until now rested on
    imaging and neuropsychological evidence alone.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:42538472
    reference_title: "Central nervous system histopathological findings in classic infantile Pompe disease: a systematic review with clinical relevance."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Histologic abnormalities were found throughout the CNS. The cerebral and cerebellar white matter (WM), globus pallidus, dentate nucleus, motor nuclei of the brainstem, and anterior horn cells were most frequently affected."
    explanation: Gives the regional distribution of CNS histologic abnormality in autopsied classic infantile patients.
  - reference: PMID:42538472
    reference_title: "Central nervous system histopathological findings in classic infantile Pompe disease: a systematic review with clinical relevance."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Cortical neurons were affected with greater variability, and subcortical structures were more frequently involved than cortical ones."
    explanation: Records the subcortical-over-cortical gradient, which is what makes this a white-matter and deep-nucleus pattern rather than a diffuse encephalopathy.
- name: Severe glycogen accumulation in astrocytes and globus pallidus neurons
  description: >-
    Within that CNS pathology, glycogen accumulation was severe specifically in
    astrocytes and in globus pallidus neurons. This is the storage lesion itself
    demonstrated in brain tissue from this patient population, rather than inferred
    from the muscle phenotype - the cell-type-level evidence that the enzyme
    deficiency produces the same lysosomal storage in the brain that it produces in
    muscle.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:42538472

- name: CNS pathology present in infancy, years before clinical or radiological signs
  description: >-
    Autopsy pathology is already early and widespread in children up to 21 months,
    whereas MRI abnormality typically appears from 2 to 3 years, biomarker change
    from around 5 years, and cognitive decline from roughly 8 to 10 years. The
    pathology therefore precedes every clinical means of detecting it by years. This
    matters for the timing of therapy rather than only for description: by the time
    CNS involvement is measurable in a treated survivor, the storage lesion is long
    established.
  diagnostic: false
  evidence:

    reference_title: "Central nervous system histopathological findings in classic infantile Pompe disease: a systematic review with clinical relevance."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Histopathological studies from children up to 21 months demonstrate early and widespread CNS pathology."
    explanation: Establishes that the CNS lesion is present in infancy.
  - reference: PMID:42538472
    reference_title: "Central nervous system histopathological findings in classic infantile Pompe disease: a systematic review with clinical relevance."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "While white matter pathology is already prominent in young patients, abnormalities on magnetic resonance imaging typically emerge from 2 to 3 years of age, biomarker changes from around 5 years, and cognitive decline, reduced processing speed, and background activity slowing on electroencephalogram from approximately 8-10 years onward."
    explanation: Quantifies the lag between the pathology and each downstream means of detecting it.
genetic:
- name: GAA
  notes: >
    Biallelic fully deleterious GAA variants cause IOPD. The specific pair of alleles
    also determines CRIM status: two alleles that produce no protein at all yield
    CRIM-negative status, whereas alleles producing a catalytically inactive protein
    yield CRIM-positive status. CRIM status can in most cases be predicted from the
    GAA genotype, avoiding a skin biopsy and the weeks of fibroblast culture that
    Western-blot CRIM testing requires - a decisive advantage in a disease where
    treatment delay is measured in weeks.

    Locus and allelic spectrum. GAA lies at 17q25.2-q25.3 and contains 20 exons. The
    allelic spectrum is highly heterogeneous - missense, nonsense, splice-site,
    partial deletions and insertions - and the Pompe disease variant database lists
    well over 900 disease-associated variants, so IOPD genotypes are typically
    private compound heterozygous pairs rather than recurrent founder alleles.

    Copy-number variants are a real diagnostic blind spot. Large deletions are about
    1.5% of known GAA variants and are invisible to exon sequencing, so when
    sequencing returns fewer than two explanatory alleles in a patient with deficient
    enzyme activity, MLPA or another dosage assay should be run before the genetic
    diagnosis is called incomplete.
  gene_term:
    preferred_term: GAA
    term:
      id: hgnc:4065
      label: GAA
  relationship_type: CAUSATIVE
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:12897283
    reference_title: "The natural course of infantile Pompe's disease: 20 original cases compared with 133 cases from the literature."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The patients have fully deleterious mutations. Acid alpha-glucosidase activity is severely deficient."
    explanation: Establishes fully deleterious biallelic GAA genotypes as the genetic basis of classic infantile disease.
  - reference: PMID:22252923
    reference_title: "Predicting cross-reactive immunological material (CRIM) status in Pompe disease using GAA mutations: lessons learned from 10 years of clinical laboratory testing experience."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "We found that, in most cases, CRIM status can be predicted from GAA mutations, potentially circumventing the need for invasive skin biopsy and time wasted in culturing cells in the future."
    explanation: Supports genotype-based prediction of CRIM status from GAA variants.
  - reference: PMID:39482698
    reference_title: "The European reference network for metabolic diseases (MetabERN) clinical pathway recommendations for Pompe disease (acid maltase deficiency, glycogen storage disease type II)."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "The GAA gene is localized on chromosome 17 at the 17q25.2–q25.3 locus and contains 20 exons including the 19 coding ones"
    explanation: Establishes the GAA locus and exon structure.
  - reference: PMID:39482698
    reference_title: "The European reference network for metabolic diseases (MetabERN) clinical pathway recommendations for Pompe disease (acid maltase deficiency, glycogen storage disease type II)."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "There is a high allelic heterogeneity/diversity: missense, nonsense, splice-site variants, partial deletions, and insertions have been reported to be causative of the disease."
    explanation: Documents the breadth of GAA variant classes underlying the disease.
  - reference: PMID:39273088
    reference_title: "Mutation Spectrum of GAA Gene in Pompe Disease: Current Knowledge and Results of an Italian Study."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "For MLPA, large deletions make up only 1.5% of known variants, and the deletion of exon 18 was a common variant in Caucasian patients."
    explanation: Quantifies the large-deletion share of the GAA allelic spectrum, which is the fraction missed by sequencing alone.
  - reference: PMID:39273088
    reference_title: "Mutation Spectrum of GAA Gene in Pompe Disease: Current Knowledge and Results of an Italian Study."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "This method revealed in four subjects the presence of exonic deletions in the GAA gene causative of PD in association with other causative point mutations."
    explanation: Demonstrates that MLPA recovers causative exonic deletions in patients whose sequencing found only one explanatory allele, supporting the dosage-assay recommendation.

- name: Acid alpha-glucosidase enzyme activity
  context: >
    Diagnosis rests on deficient GAA enzyme activity in isolated lymphocytes or mixed
    leukocytes (or fibroblasts/muscle), with classic infantile patients showing
    severely deficient activity, typically less than 1% of the control mean.

- name: Urinary glucose tetrasaccharide (Glc4/Hex4)
  context: >
    Urinary glucose tetrasaccharide is a glycogen-burden biomarker used to monitor
    treatment response in IOPD. Together with creatine kinase, low levels correlate
    with favourable response to enzyme replacement therapy, and uGlc4 is the more
    stable of the two.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:32373469
    reference_title: "Earlier and higher dosing of alglucosidase alfa improve outcomes in patients with infantile-onset Pompe disease: Evidence from real-world experiences."
    supports: SUPPORT

    snippet: "Low CK and uGlc4 levels were correlated with favorable response to ERT in IOPD patients, although CK may be more fluctuated than uGlc4."
    explanation: Establishes urinary Glc4 as a monitoring biomarker of ERT response in IOPD.
  - reference: PMID:15886040
    reference_title: "Glucose tetrasaccharide as a biomarker for monitoring the therapeutic response to enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Both urinary Glc4 and plasma Hex4 levels decreased after initiation of ERT for all patients."
    explanation: Establishes the direction of the biomarker's response to enzyme replacement therapy - it falls in every treated patient, so a fall is not by itself a marker of good response.
  - reference: PMID:15886040
    reference_title: "Glucose tetrasaccharide as a biomarker for monitoring the therapeutic response to enzyme replacement therapy for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In contrast, levels fluctuated and were persistently elevated above the control ranges in those patients with a less favorable clinical response"
    explanation: Supplies the discriminating observation - persistent elevation above the control range, rather than the initial fall, is what marks a poorer clinical response.
- name: Anti-rhGAA IgG antibody titer
  context: >
    Serial anti-recombinant-human-GAA IgG titers stratify treatment response.
    CRIM-negative infants develop earlier, higher, and more sustained titers; by
    contrast, long-term survivors of IOPD had low or undetectable anti-alglucosidase
    alfa antibody titers.
  evidence:

    explanation: Reports the antibody-titer difference by CRIM status that makes this a decision-relevant biomarker.
  - reference: PMID:22538254

    supports: SUPPORT

    snippet: "All long-term survivors had low or undetectable anti-alglucosidase alfa antibody titers."
    explanation: Shows the association between low antibody titer and long-term survival on ERT.
treatments:
- name: Enzyme replacement therapy with alglucosidase alfa
  description: >
    Intravenous recombinant human GAA is the disease-specific therapy for IOPD.
    Initiated before age six months and before the need for ventilatory assistance, it
    improves survival and ventilator-independent survival, reduces cardiac mass, and
    substantially improves motor skill acquisition. High-dose ERT started immediately
    on a newborn-screening diagnosis gives the best reported outcomes.
  therapeutic_modality: PROTEIN_REPLACEMENT
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: enzyme replacement therapy
    term:
      id: NCIT:C16221
      label: Protein Replacement Therapy
    therapeutic_agent:
    - preferred_term: alglucosidase alfa
      term:
        id: NCIT:C65221
        label: Alglucosidase Alfa
  target_phenotypes:

      label: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy

    term:
      id: HP:0001270
      label: Motor delay
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Lysosomal glycogen accumulation
    treatment_effect: INHIBITS
    description: Infused recombinant human GAA supplies the missing lysosomal enzyme and clears stored glycogen.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:20301438
      reference_title: "Pompe Disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: OTHER
      snippet: "In those in whom enzyme replacement therapy (ERT) is initiated before age six months and before the need for ventilatory assistance, a majority have improved survival, improved ventilator-independent survival, reduced cardiac mass, and significantly improved acquisition of motor skills compared to untreated individuals."
      explanation: Reduced cardiac mass and improved survival evidence the reversal of the storage lesion by ERT.

    supports: SUPPORT

    snippet: "All patients (100%) survived to 18 months of age. A Cox proportional hazards analysis demonstrated that treatment reduced the risk of death by 99%, reduced the risk of death or invasive ventilation by 92%, and reduced the risk of death or any type of ventilation by 88%, as compared to an untreated historical control group."
    explanation: Pivotal trial quantifies the survival benefit of rhGAA in infantile-onset disease.

    explanation: Real-world cohort supports early, high-dose ERT initiation at newborn screening.

    explanation: Quantifies the penalty of delayed ERT initiation on motor outcome in classic IOPD.
- name: Avalglucosidase alfa
  description: >
    A next-generation recombinant human GAA with enhanced mannose-6-phosphate receptor
    binding. In the Mini-COMET phase 2 trial, children with IOPD who had declined or
    responded suboptimally to alglucosidase alfa were switched to avalglucosidase
    alfa, with persistent normalization of left ventricular mass z score, falling
    disease-burden biomarkers, and improved or stable motor function through 97 weeks.

- name: Immune tolerance induction with rituximab and methotrexate
  description: >
    Because CRIM-negative infants neutralize rhGAA, immune tolerance induction is
    given prophylactically at ERT initiation, or therapeutically once antibodies
    appear. Rituximab (anti-CD20 B-cell depletion) plus methotrexate, with or without
    intravenous immunoglobulin, eliminated established anti-rhGAA antibody and
    conferred durable tolerance off all immune therapy with B-cell recovery. It is
    most effective before an entrenched high-titer response develops, which is why
    CRIM status should be known before the first infusion.
  therapeutic_modality: MONOCLONAL_ANTIBODY
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: immune tolerance induction
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15986
      label: Pharmacotherapy
    therapeutic_agent:
    - preferred_term: rituximab
      term:
        id: NCIT:C1702
        label: Rituximab
    - preferred_term: methotrexate
      term:
        id: NCIT:C642
        label: Methotrexate
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: High sustained anti-rhGAA antibody response
    treatment_effect: INHIBITS
    description: B-cell depletion plus antimetabolite immunomodulation prevents or eliminates the neutralizing anti-rhGAA IgG response.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:22237443
      reference_title: "Successful immune tolerance induction to enzyme replacement therapy in CRIM-negative infantile Pompe disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "In both patients treated therapeutically, anti-rhGAA was eliminated after 3 and 19 months. All four patients are immune tolerant to rhGAA, off immune therapy, showing B-cell recovery while continuing to receive ERT"
      explanation: Directly evidences elimination of the antibody response and durable tolerance.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:22237443
    reference_title: "Successful immune tolerance induction to enzyme replacement therapy in CRIM-negative infantile Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "All patients show clinical response to ERT, in stark contrast to the rapid deterioration of their nontolerized CRIM-negative counterparts."
    explanation: Contrasts tolerized with non-tolerized CRIM-negative infants, supporting the clinical value of tolerance induction.
  - reference: PMID:20301438
    reference_title: "Pompe Disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "individuals who do not produce CRIM (i.e., who are CRIM negative) generally develop high titer anti-rhGAA antibodies during ERT and require modified therapy protocols using immunomodulation early in the treatment course, optimally before the first infusion."
    explanation: Management guidance specifying immunomodulation for CRIM-negative infants, optimally before the first infusion.

- name: AAV9-mediated GAA gene therapy
  description: >-
    A single intravenous infusion of an adeno-associated virus serotype 9 vector
    carrying codon-optimized human GAA cDNA, given at 1.2 x 10^14 vector genomes per
    kilogram. In a reported infantile-onset series, three of four treated
    patients had improved cardiac outcomes and motor function over 52 weeks and no
    anti-GAA antibodies were detected; one patient was withdrawn and subsequently
    died. The rationale is that a single administration of a gene that the body then
    expresses avoids both the every-other-week infusion burden of enzyme replacement
    and the anti-rhGAA antibody response that defeats it in CRIM-negative infants -
    but this is an early-phase result in four patients, not established therapy.
  therapeutic_modality: GENE_THERAPY
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: AAV9-delivered GAA gene transfer
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15238
      label: Gene Therapy
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Near-complete acid alpha-glucosidase deficiency
    treatment_effect: RESTORES
    description: >-
      Vector-delivered GAA cDNA supplies the missing enzyme from the patient's own
      transduced cells, addressing the initiating lesion rather than a downstream
      consequence.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:40561529
      reference_title: "AAV9-Mediated Gene Therapy for Infantile-Onset Pompe's Disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "Four patients with infantile-onset Pompe's disease received a single intravenous injection of an adeno-associated virus serotype 9 vector carrying codon-optimized complementary DNA encoding human acid α-glucosidase (GAA)"
      explanation: Describes the intervention acting on the enzyme-deficiency node in infantile-onset patients.
  target_phenotypes:
  - preferred_term: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy
    term:
      id: HP:0001639
      label: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:40561529
    reference_title: "AAV9-Mediated Gene Therapy for Infantile-Onset Pompe's Disease."
    supports: SUPPORT

    snippet: "The other patients had improvement in cardiac outcomes and motor function over a 52-week observation period."
    explanation: Reports the cardiac and motor benefit observed in the treated infantile-onset patients.
  - reference: PMID:40561529
    reference_title: "AAV9-Mediated Gene Therapy for Infantile-Onset Pompe's Disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Anti-GAA antibodies were not detected in any of the patients during the observation period."
    explanation: Reports that no anti-GAA antibody response was detected over the observation period. The publication does not report the CRIM status of these four patients, so this does not by itself speak to the CRIM-negative antibody-mediated failure curated elsewhere in this entry.
  - reference: PMID:40561529
    reference_title: "AAV9-Mediated Gene Therapy for Infantile-Onset Pompe's Disease."
    supports: REFUTE
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "One patient was withdrawn from the study and subsequently died."
    explanation: Records the outcome in the fourth patient, which counts against reading this four-patient series as an established benefit.
  - reference: PMID:42142433
    reference_title: "Sensory neuronopathy secondary to gene therapy with AT845 for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Injury to dorsal root ganglion neurons has been reported in animal models treated with gene therapy using recombinant AAV, but there are limited reports of AAV-mediated sensory neuronopathy in clinical trials."
    explanation: Records dorsal-root-ganglion toxicity as a recognised class risk of recombinant AAV gene therapy, and states plainly how thin the clinical evidence for it currently is.
  - reference: PMID:42142433
    reference_title: "Sensory neuronopathy secondary to gene therapy with AT845 for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "We describe the clinical, electrophysiological and response to treatment features of a 49-year-old woman with Pompe disease who developed a sensory neuronopathy after receiving AT845, an investigational adeno-associated virus (AAV) gene replacement therapy"
    explanation: The single reported case grounding that class risk in Pompe disease specifically. Note the scope limits, which are why this is a class-level caution rather than a finding about the treatment curated here - the patient was an adult with late-onset disease, and the product was AT845, not the AAV9-GAA vector this entry curates.
  - reference: PMID:37463048
    reference_title: "AAV-mediated delivery of secreted acid α-glucosidase with enhanced uptake corrects neuromuscular pathology in Pompe mice."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "adeno-associated virus-mediated (AAV-mediated) systemic gene transfer reversed glycogen storage in all key therapeutic targets - skeletal and cardiac muscles, the diaphragm, and the central nervous system - in both young and severely affected old Gaa-knockout mice"
    explanation: The preclinical basis for systemic AAV in this disease, and the reason it is of interest beyond enzyme replacement therapy - it reversed storage in the central nervous system, which infused enzyme does not reach.
  - reference: PMID:37463048
    reference_title: "AAV-mediated delivery of secreted acid α-glucosidase with enhanced uptake corrects neuromuscular pathology in Pompe mice."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "the therapy reversed secondary cellular abnormalities in skeletal muscle, such as those in autophagy and mTORC1/AMPK signaling"
    explanation: Shows the same intervention reversing the autophagic abnormality curated as a pathophysiology node in this entry, not only the glycogen storage upstream of it.
  notes: >-
    Investigational. The series is four patients in a single-arm Phase 1 study
    (ChiCTR2200063229, curated under clinical_trials), followed for 52 weeks, so it
    establishes neither durability nor a survival benefit over enzyme replacement
    therapy.
diagnosis:
- name: Newborn screening for GAA deficiency
  description: >-
    Pompe disease is screened in newborns by measuring acid alpha-glucosidase
    activity in dried blood spots. The European clinical pathway calls newborn
    screening essential specifically for the infantile-onset forms, and this entry's
    own histopathology section supplies the tissue-level reason: the amount of damage
    already present at baseline limits what enzyme replacement therapy can undo, so
    identifying an affected infant before symptoms appear is what decides the
    outcome. The dried blood spot assay is a screening test only - a low result needs
    confirmation, and sequencing also yields the genotype used to predict CRIM status
    before enzyme replacement therapy is started.
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: newborn dried blood spot screening for GAA deficiency
    term:
      id: NCIT:C81178
      label: Newborn Screening
  results: >-
    A low dried-blood-spot GAA activity result must be confirmed before it is treated
    as a diagnosis; confirmation is by enzyme assay in leukocytes and/or biallelic
    pathogenic GAA variants on molecular testing.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:39482698
    reference_title: "The European reference network for metabolic diseases (MetabERN) clinical pathway recommendations for Pompe disease (acid maltase deficiency, glycogen storage disease type II)."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Newborn screening (NBS) for Pompe disease is possible by measuring GAA activity in dried blood spots with different methods (tandem-mass spectrometry, fluorometry, microfluidics)"
    explanation: States the assay on which newborn screening for this disease is based.
  - reference: PMID:39482698
    reference_title: "The European reference network for metabolic diseases (MetabERN) clinical pathway recommendations for Pompe disease (acid maltase deficiency, glycogen storage disease type II)."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Newborn screening is essential for timely identification and treatment of patients with the infantile-onset forms of the disease."
    explanation: The European clinical pathway singles out the infantile-onset forms as the ones for which newborn screening is essential, which is what makes this a diagnosis entry on this disease rather than on Pompe disease generally.
  - reference: PMID:39482698
    reference_title: "The European reference network for metabolic diseases (MetabERN) clinical pathway recommendations for Pompe disease (acid maltase deficiency, glycogen storage disease type II)."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "the assay in dried blood spots is only a screening test and is not sufficient for definitive diagnosis"
    explanation: Records the limitation that makes confirmatory testing mandatory rather than optional.
  - reference: PMID:20301438
    reference_title: "Pompe Disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "identification of biallelic pathogenic (or likely pathogenic) variants in GAA by molecular genetic testing in a proband with an out-of-range newborn screening result and/or suggestive clinical features"
    explanation: GeneReviews states what confirms the diagnosis after an out-of-range newborn screening result.
  - reference: PMID:42320386
    reference_title: "High prevalence of GAA c.[752C > T;761C > T] haplotype complicates high-risk screening for Pompe disease in the Chinese population."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Pseudodeficiency alleles pose a significant challenge to Pompe disease (PD) newborn and high-risk screening by causing false-positive enzyme reductions without clinical manifestations."
    explanation: Names the dominant false-positive mechanism in newborn screening for this disease, which is why a low dried-blood-spot result is not a diagnosis.
  - reference: PMID:42320386
    reference_title: "High prevalence of GAA c.[752C > T;761C > T] haplotype complicates high-risk screening for Pompe disease in the Chinese population."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "individuals homozygous for the haplotype or carrying it in trans with a single pathogenic variant remained asymptomatic, despite dried blood spot (DBS) GAA activity falling below the diagnostic threshold"
    explanation: Shows the failure mode concretely - enzyme activity below the screening threshold in people who never develop the disease - which is what second-tier genotyping is for.
  - reference: PMID:42320386
    reference_title: "High prevalence of GAA c.[752C > T;761C > T] haplotype complicates high-risk screening for Pompe disease in the Chinese population."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "the haplotype is markedly enriched in East Asian populations"
    explanation: Records that the false-positive burden is ancestry-stratified, so screening performance is not uniform across populations.
- name: CRIM status determination before starting enzyme replacement therapy
  description: >-
    Cross-reactive immunologic material status is established before enzyme
    replacement therapy begins, because it decides whether prophylactic immune
    tolerance induction is needed alongside it. A systematic review addressed how
    CRIM status is determined in practice, how CRIM-negative status affects outcome,
    and which immune tolerance induction regimens are actually used. CRIM status is
    not a measure of enzyme activity: both CRIM-positive and CRIM-negative infants
    have essentially none, and the question is whether any GAA protein exists for the
    immune system to recognise as self.
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: CRIM status determination
    term:
      id: NCIT:C18020
      label: Diagnostic Procedure
  results: >-
    A CRIM-negative result calls for prophylactic immune tolerance induction started
    with, or before, enzyme replacement therapy rather than after antibodies appear.
    The review reports that starting immune tolerance induction at or before the first
    enzyme dose improves prognosis in CRIM-positive patients as well, so a
    CRIM-positive result is not by itself a reason to withhold it.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41583481
    reference_title: "Insights into immunogenicity and therapeutic strategies to mitigate the immune response in infantile-onset Pompe disease: a comprehensive systematic literature review."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "CRIM status should be determined before initiating ERT since CRIM-negative patients have a very high risk of developing an immune response against ERT, potentially leading to treatment failure"
    explanation: States the claim this entry makes - that CRIM status is established before enzyme replacement therapy starts - and the reason, which is the risk of an immune response that defeats the therapy.
  - reference: PMID:41583481
    reference_title: "Insights into immunogenicity and therapeutic strategies to mitigate the immune response in infantile-onset Pompe disease: a comprehensive systematic literature review."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Initiating ITI therapy concurrent or prior to the first dose of ERT can improve the prognosis of patients with IOPD with either CRIM-negative or CRIM-positive status"
    explanation: "Supports the results field - immune tolerance induction started with or before the first enzyme dose rather than after antibodies appear. Note the scope is wider than the results text; the review reports benefit for CRIM-positive patients too, not only CRIM-negative ones."
  - reference: PMID:41583481
    reference_title: "Insights into immunogenicity and therapeutic strategies to mitigate the immune response in infantile-onset Pompe disease: a comprehensive systematic literature review."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Recently, there has been a major shift from direct CRIM testing using western blotting and mutation analysis to CRIM status prediction based on genetic variant analysis."
    explanation: Records how the determination is actually made now, which is why the binding here is the generic Diagnostic Procedure rather than a protein-assay or a genetic-testing term - both routes are in use.

discussions:
- discussion_id: gap_iopd_cns_glycogen_white_matter
  kind: KNOWLEDGE_GAP
  status: OPEN
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Central nervous system glycogen storage and progressive white matter abnormality
  prompt: >-
    Does the progressive cerebral white-matter abnormality of ERT-treated IOPD
    survivors reflect ongoing CNS glycogen storage that intravenous rhGAA cannot
    reach, and would a brain-penetrant therapy prevent it?
  rationale: >-
    Autopsy evidence now establishes the storage side of this question. A systematic
    review of 49 classic infantile patients found severe glycogen accumulation in
    astrocytes and globus pallidus neurons, with white matter among the most
    frequently affected regions, so CNS glycogen storage is no longer an inference
    from the muscle phenotype. What remains open is the link between that storage and
    the clinical trajectory: the review states directly that the mechanisms
    connecting glycogen accumulation to neurological manifestations are still
    incompletely understood, and there is still no interventional evidence that
    reducing CNS glycogen alters the neuropsychological outcome. The same review
    sharpens the gap rather than closing it, by adding a timing problem that was not
    previously part of it - pathology is already widespread before 21 months, whereas
    MRI change appears from 2 to 3 years, biomarker change from about 5 years, and
    cognitive decline from about 8 to 10, so any causal account has to explain that
    lag and any trial of a brain-directed therapy has to contend with an endpoint
    that trails the lesion by years. Preclinical support exists - systemic AAV
    reversed CNS glycogen storage in Gaa-knockout mice (PMID:37463048, cited on the
    gene-therapy treatment) - but that has not been shown to change neurological
    outcome in humans.
  proposed_experiments:
  - experiment_id: exp_iopd_cns_glycogen_imaging
    name: Longitudinal CNS glycogen and white-matter imaging in treated IOPD
    description: >-
      Correlate a CNS glycogen-burden measure with serial quantitative
      white-matter MRI and neuropsychological testing in a multi-centre treated
      IOPD cohort, and compare against recipients of brain-directed enzyme
      delivery when such therapy becomes available.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:29573408
    reference_title: "Classic infantile Pompe patients approaching adulthood: a cohort study on consequences for the brain."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: >-
      Therefore, we advise follow-up programs are expanded to capture CNS involvement in larger, international patient cohorts, to incorporate our findings in the counselling of parents before the start of treatment, and to include the brain as an additional target in the development of next-generation therapeutic strategies for classic infantile Pompe disease.
    explanation: The authors frame CNS involvement as an open target rather than a resolved mechanism.
  - reference: PMID:42538472
    reference_title: "Central nervous system histopathological findings in classic infantile Pompe disease: a systematic review with clinical relevance."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The mechanisms linking glycogen accumulation to neurological manifestations remain incompletely understood."
    explanation: The systematic review states that the storage-to-manifestation link is still unresolved, which is what keeps this gap OPEN despite the new tissue-level storage evidence.
  - reference: PMID:42538472
    reference_title: "Central nervous system histopathological findings in classic infantile Pompe disease: a systematic review with clinical relevance."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "there is a marked temporal gap between the extensive abnormalities observed at autopsy in infancy and later radiological, biochemical, and clinical manifestations"
    explanation: Adds the timing dimension to this gap - the pathology precedes every clinical means of measuring it by years, which any mechanistic account and any trial endpoint has to account for.

animal_models:
- name: GAA-knockout mouse
  species: Mouse
  genotype: Gaa knockout (GAA-null)
  publication: PMID:20040311
  description: >
    The GAA-knockout mouse reproduces the lysosomal glycogen storage lesion and was
    the system in which the autophagic-buildup arm of Pompe muscle pathology, and its
    interference with enzyme replacement therapy, was established.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:20040311
    reference_title: "Autophagy in skeletal muscle: implications for Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "Not only does this build-up of autophagosomes disrupt the contractile apparatus in the muscle fibers, it also interferes with enzyme replacement therapy by acting as a sink for the recombinant enzyme and preventing its efficient delivery to the lysosomes."
    explanation: Reports the knockout-mouse finding that makes this model informative here - autophagic buildup both damages the contractile apparatus and sequesters infused recombinant enzyme.
  modeled_mechanisms:
  - target: Failure of productive autophagy and autophagic buildup
    relationship: RECAPITULATES
    fidelity: MODERATE
    description: >
      Progressive accumulation of autophagic vesicles in type II-rich fibers, with
      sequestration of infused recombinant enzyme.
    limitations: >-
      The autophagic buildup in this model is restricted to type II-rich fibers, and
      mouse and human skeletal muscle differ in fiber-type composition, so the
      proportion of muscle affected does not transfer directly. The model is also not
      specific to the infantile form: it models GAA deficiency, not the age of onset
      or cardiac severity that defines IOPD, and it says nothing about CRIM status,
      the other determinant of ERT response in infants.
    readouts:
    - name: Autophagic vesicle accumulation in type II-rich muscle fibers
      target: Failure of productive autophagy and autophagic buildup
      direction: INCREASED
      interpretation: Histological correlate of the autophagic-buildup node in this model.
      evidence:
      - reference: PMID:20040311
        reference_title: "Autophagy in skeletal muscle: implications for Pompe disease."
        supports: SUPPORT
        evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
        snippet: "In the GAA-knockout mouse model, progressive accumulation of autophagic vesicles is restricted to Type II-rich muscle fibers."
        explanation: Reports the measurement and its direction in the knockout mouse.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:20040311
      reference_title: "Autophagy in skeletal muscle: implications for Pompe disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
      snippet: "we present evidence that a failure of productive autophagy in muscle tissue contributes strongly to disease pathology in both patients with Pompe disease and GAA-knockout mice"
      explanation: Supports treating the knockout mouse as informative for the autophagy node in human disease.
- name: Lapphund canine Pompe disease (naturally occurring GAA p.W746* homozygote)
  species: Dog
  genotype: GAA c.2237G>A (premature stop at residue 746) homozygous
  publication: PMID:23457621
  description: >-
    A naturally occurring canine Pompe disease found in Finnish Lapphunds, Swedish
    Lapphunds, and Lapponian Herders. The dogs are homozygous for a GAA nonsense
    variant at the codon whose human counterpart causes infantile disease when paired
    with a second fully deleterious allele, and they reproduce the infantile form
    both genetically and clinico-pathologically. Unlike the engineered mouse models,
    this is a spontaneous large-animal disease, which is what makes it the preferred
    system for preclinical gene-therapy work.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:23457621
    reference_title: "A nonsense mutation in the acid α-glucosidase gene causes Pompe disease in Finnish and Swedish Lapphunds."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "The affected dogs are homozygous for the GAA c.2237G>A mutation leading to a premature stop codon at amino acid position 746."
    explanation: Establishes the causal genotype of this naturally occurring canine disease, which is what makes it a model of GAA deficiency rather than an unrelated canine myopathy.
  modeled_mechanisms:
  - target: Near-complete acid alpha-glucosidase deficiency
    relationship: RECAPITULATES
    fidelity: MODERATE
    description: >-
      A homozygous premature stop codon abolishes acid alpha-glucosidase, the same
      class of fully deleterious lesion that produces classic infantile disease in
      humans.
    limitations: >-
      The dogs are homozygous for one nonsense allele, whereas human infantile-onset
      disease usually arises from two different deleterious alleles, so the model
      does not reproduce the human allelic heterogeneity or its effect on residual
      protein. The publication reports genetic and clinico-pathological similarity
      to the human infantile form but does not itself establish CRIM status or the
      anti-rhGAA antibody behaviour that governs enzyme replacement response in
      infants, so it says nothing about that axis of the disease.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:23457621
      reference_title: "A nonsense mutation in the acid α-glucosidase gene causes Pompe disease in Finnish and Swedish Lapphunds."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
      snippet: "The affected dogs from both the Finnish as well as the Swedish breed mimic infantile-onset Pompe disease genetically, but also clinico-pathologically."
      explanation: States that this naturally occurring canine disease reproduces the human infantile-onset form at both the genetic and the pathological level.
    - reference: PMID:23457621
      reference_title: "A nonsense mutation in the acid α-glucosidase gene causes Pompe disease in Finnish and Swedish Lapphunds."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
      snippet: "Therefore this canine model provides a valuable tool for preclinical studies aimed at the development of gene therapy in Pompe disease."
      explanation: Supports treating the canine disease as a preclinical model, which is the use this entry makes of it alongside the gene-therapy treatment.
- name: Gaa c.1935C>A (p.D645E) knock-in mouse
  species: Mouse
  genotype: Gaaem1935C>A (p.Asp645Glu) homozygous knock-in
  publication: PMID:36517654
  description: >-
    A CRISPR-Cas9 knock-in mouse carrying the human GAA c.1935C>A (p.Asp645Glu)
    variant, which is the most frequent infantile-onset allele in people of Southern
    Han Chinese ancestry. By three months these mice have both skeletal muscle
    weakness and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, the two features that define the human
    infantile form, which is the basis on which the authors present it as a model of
    the infantile-onset disease rather than of GAA deficiency in general.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:36517654
    reference_title: "CRISPR-mediated generation and characterization of a Gaa homozygous c.1935C>A (p.D645E) Pompe disease knock-in mouse model recapitulating human infantile onset-Pompe disease."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "The c.1935C>A (p.Asp645Glu) variant, the most frequent GAA pathogenic mutation in people of Southern Han Chinese ancestry, causes infantile-onset Pompe disease (IOPD)"
    explanation: Establishes that the knocked-in allele is itself a human infantile-onset-causing variant, which is the basis for treating this line as an IOPD model rather than a generic GAA-deficiency model.
  modeled_mechanisms:
  - target: Cardiomyocyte glycogen storage and progressive hypertrophic remodeling
    relationship: RECAPITULATES
    fidelity: MODERATE
    description: >-
      Homozygous knock-in mice develop hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, the cardiac
      feature that separates infantile-onset from late-onset disease.
    limitations: >-
      The mice show no premature mortality, so the model reproduces the cardiac and
      muscle lesions without the fatal course that defines the untreated human
      disease. It also models one specific missense allele rather than the null
      genotypes behind most classic infantile disease, and the characterization
      reported is at a single three-month timepoint.
    readouts:
    - name: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and skeletal muscle weakness at 3 months
      target: Cardiomyocyte glycogen storage and progressive hypertrophic remodeling
      direction: INCREASED
      interpretation: Presence of the defining cardiac lesion of infantile-onset disease in the knock-in model.
      evidence:
      - reference: PMID:36517654
        reference_title: "CRISPR-mediated generation and characterization of a Gaa homozygous c.1935C>A (p.D645E) Pompe disease knock-in mouse model recapitulating human infantile onset-Pompe disease."
        supports: SUPPORT
        evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
        snippet: "Three-month-old mice demonstrated skeletal muscle weakness and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy but no premature mortality."
        explanation: Reports the measurement, its direction, and the negative survival result in the same sentence.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:36517654
      reference_title: "CRISPR-mediated generation and characterization of a Gaa homozygous c.1935C>A (p.D645E) Pompe disease knock-in mouse model recapitulating human infantile onset-Pompe disease."
      supports: SUPPORT

      explanation: Supports treating this knock-in line as informative for the infantile-onset cardiac phenotype.
  - target: Failure of productive autophagy and autophagic buildup
    relationship: RECAPITULATES
    fidelity: MODERATE
    description: >-
      The knock-in mice have near-abolished GAA activity with markedly increased
      tissue glycogen and impaired autophagy, reproducing the autophagy arm of the
      mechanism in an allele-specific rather than a null background.
    limitations: >-
      Autophagy is reported as impaired at three months without the longitudinal
      characterization available for the knockout line, and the mice do not progress
      to the fatal outcome, so the model does not show how far the autophagic lesion
      goes in untreated disease.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:36517654
      reference_title: "CRISPR-mediated generation and characterization of a Gaa homozygous c.1935C>A (p.D645E) Pompe disease knock-in mouse model recapitulating human infantile onset-Pompe disease."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
      snippet: "Homozygous Gaaem1935C>A knock-in mice exhibited normal Gaa mRNA expression levels relative to wild-type mice, had near-abolished GAA enzymatic activity, markedly increased tissue glycogen storage, and concomitantly impaired autophagy."
      explanation: Reports abolished enzyme activity, glycogen storage, and impaired autophagy together in this model.
experimental_models:
- name: Patient-derived iPSC cardiomyocytes (PD-iCMs)
  description: >-
    Cardiomyocytes differentiated from Pompe patient induced pluripotent stem cells.
    They reproduce the cardiac storage lesion in a human genetic background -
    decreased GAA activity, lysosomal glycogen accumulation, and hypertrophy - and
    were used to show that mitochondrial dysfunction accompanies the hypertrophy, in
    human cardiomyocytes carrying patient genotypes rather than in a rodent
    background. Supplementing the
    cultures with recombinant human GAA improved mitochondrial function and mitigated
    the cardiac pathology, making this a system in which the disease lesion and its
    response to the actual clinical therapy are both observable.
  experimental_model_type: IPSC_DERIVED_MODEL
  organism:
    preferred_term: human
    term:
      id: NCBITaxon:9606
      label: Homo sapiens
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: iPSC-derived cardiomyocyte
    term:
      id: CL:0000746
      label: cardiac muscle cell
  cell_source: Patient-derived induced pluripotent stem cells
  publication: PMID:37916452
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:37916452
    reference_title: "Mitochondrial dysfunction is associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in Pompe disease-specific induced pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: IN_VITRO
    snippet: "Therefore, this study suggests that defective mitochondria underlie the pathogenesis of cardiomyopathy in patients with PD."
    explanation: States the conclusion this model system was built to support, and so what the model is being curated as evidence for.
  modeled_mechanisms:
  - target: Cardiomyocyte glycogen storage and progressive hypertrophic remodeling
    relationship: RECAPITULATES
    fidelity: MODERATE
    description: >-
      The differentiated cardiomyocytes show reduced GAA activity, lysosomal glycogen
      accumulation, and hypertrophy - the cellular content of this node - in human
      cells carrying patient genotypes.
    limitations: >-
      iPSC-derived cardiomyocytes are immature relative to adult myocardium and are
      cultured without haemodynamic load, so the hypertrophy they show is not the
      pressure-loaded ventricular remodeling of the intact infant heart. The source
      publication describes the donors as Pompe disease patients without stating that
      they are the classic infantile form, so the genotype-to-onset link is not
      established by this study alone.
    readouts:
    - name: Mitochondrial number, respiratory function, and ATP production
      target: Cardiomyocyte glycogen storage and progressive hypertrophic remodeling
      direction: DECREASED
      interpretation: >-
        Mitochondrial deficit accompanying the storage and hypertrophy phenotype in
        patient-derived cardiomyocytes.
      evidence:
      - reference: PMID:37916452
        reference_title: "Mitochondrial dysfunction is associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in Pompe disease-specific induced pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes."
        supports: SUPPORT
        evidence_source: IN_VITRO
        snippet: "The defective mitochondria were involved in the cardiac pathology as shown by the significantly decreased number of mitochondria and impaired respiratory function and ATP production in PD-iCMs"
        explanation: Reports the mitochondrial measurements and their direction in the patient-derived cardiomyocytes.
    - name: Rescue of mitochondrial function and cardiac pathology by recombinant human GAA
      target: Cardiomyocyte glycogen storage and progressive hypertrophic remodeling
      direction: RESTORED
      interpretation: >-
        Supplying the missing enzyme partially reverses the model phenotype, which is
        the in vitro counterpart of the cardiac response to enzyme replacement therapy.
      evidence:
      - reference: PMID:37916452
        reference_title: "Mitochondrial dysfunction is associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in Pompe disease-specific induced pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes."
        supports: SUPPORT
        evidence_source: IN_VITRO
        snippet: "This was alleviated by supplementation with recombinant human acid alpha-glucosidase that improved the mitochondrial function and concomitantly mitigated the cardiac pathology."
        explanation: Reports the rescue arm and what it restored.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:37916452
      reference_title: "Mitochondrial dysfunction is associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in Pompe disease-specific induced pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes."
      supports: SUPPORT
      evidence_source: IN_VITRO
      snippet: "PD patient-specific induced pluripotent stem cells were differentiated into cardiomyocytes (PD-iCMs) that exhibited cardiomyopathic features of PD, including decreased acid alpha-glucosidase activity, lysosomal glycogen accumulation and hypertrophy"
      explanation: Supports treating these cardiomyocytes as informative for the cardiac storage and hypertrophy node.

notes: >
  Scope. This entry curates MONDO:0017694, the classic infantile-onset form of
  glycogen storage disease due to acid maltase deficiency. It is a child of
  Pompe Disease (MONDO:0009290), which remains the umbrella entry covering the
  whole GAA-deficiency continuum; the late-onset form is a separate concept
  (MONDO:0018485). Shared mechanism - GAA deficiency, lysosomal glycogen
  accumulation - is stated here because dismech entries are self-contained rather
  than inheriting, but the content that justifies a separate entry is the
  IOPD-specific material: near-total enzyme deficiency, the obligate cardiac
  phenotype, the untreated infantile natural history, the CRIM-status/anti-rhGAA
  antibody axis with its immune-tolerance-induction therapy, and the ERT-era
  long-term-survivor phenotype including CNS white-matter disease.

  Terminology caution. IOPD is defined by onset before 12 months WITH
  cardiomyopathy. Onset before 12 months WITHOUT cardiomyopathy is classified by
  GeneReviews as late-onset disease, so "infantile" in the literature does not
  always mean IOPD in this sense; some sources call the latter atypical or
  non-classical infantile disease. Clinical snippets quoted here were checked to be
  about the classic cardiac infantile form. Two classes of item deliberately are
  not, and say so where they sit: mechanism sources that describe the shared
  GAA-deficiency lesion across the whole spectrum, and model and dataset records,
  whose `limitations` and `notes` state where the model or cohort is Pompe disease
  generally rather than the infantile form.

  CRIM status is not the same as enzyme activity. Enzyme activity asks whether GAA
  works; CRIM asks whether any GAA protein exists. Both CRIM-positive and
  CRIM-negative IOPD patients have essentially no enzyme activity - the difference
  is immunological, and it is what determines whether enzyme replacement therapy
  works.
